Looking back at your two earlier ventures — well, one startup — what do you think your learning from it was? Actually, my strongest core takeaway is that you should work on the most essential thing. Over my past several ventures, I took so many detours and did so many non-essential things. Sometimes it's a mindset like, for instance, hoping that if I just hire this one person, my problem will be solved. You really can't hire with that mindset. Or thinking, if I land this customer, then I can raise funding. Not necessarily, actually. And on the product side — on the product side, actually, that's where I've learned the most. Building a product is always about doing something valuable. It sounds simple, but I think a lot of the time it's extremely hard to actually do. For example, back then, taking unmanned retail as the case — the thing is, everyone was holding a hammer looking for a nail. Like, "I have this really cool black-box technology, so I'll put it into an unmanned store to save on the cashiers themselves." But actually, when we were really building the product, we found that saving labor costs wasn't, at its core, the most valuable thing about the product. The most valuable thing turned out to be that while we were saving customers time, when they interacted with our screen, they saw value. For example, I put a snack in the cart, and it recommends another snack to me, or for instance, after I put in this snack, if I buy two more, then — you'd get a coupon. That's when we realized that the core of our product's value was that we could increase the average basket size, not how much labor we could save. And at the same time, in the process of building the product, we also discovered a really big opportunity in advertising, so the overall product value became really, really high. So that value is something that could never be achieved inside an unmanned retail store, because there you don't have a screen that can interact with you. So those are some of the lessons we learned along the way. How do you think about a more essential question instead of an immediate one, like landing one more customer or raising one more round? Right — I think landing one more customer, if your product hasn't achieved ROI, then landing one more customer is meaningless. Because landing one more customer, unless you know that in the long run, say, once you scale up, the ROI will work itself out — but if the whole business flywheel itself hasn't been built, there's no way to achieve product-market fit. A lot of people think product-market fit is just "I signed a customer, and the customer is willing to pay me a certain amount, so let's just do it." But in the robotics industry, I think there are actually a lot of companies that found so-called product-market fit, only to discover later that the customization they'd need to do, or the cycle times customers demand — they fall far short of what customers expect from the essential value of a robot, in terms of product form. So I think about this by always tracing the product back to ROI: does your ROI hold up? If your ROI doesn't hold, especially in hardware and robotics, I think if the ROI doesn't hold, it's not a product, and there's no product-market fit. And at the same time, after talking with people back then— back then we talked with laundromats— hotels and so on— and we found that it was still a very common pain point. In every hotel— every laundry room— there are dedicated folders and dedicated attendants doing these tasks. And the problem is hard enough, so for my model's— there's still a lot of room for fundamental improvement. So that's why we chose this scenario at the time. What's technically hard about it? Technically, it has to do with— for example, it— because folding clothes is a soft-body problem— Soft bodies are somewhat different from rigid bodies. A rigid body, like a cup for example— it might only have one state like the cup sitting with its opening facing up, and the other states might be it tipping over or flipping upside down. Folding is different. In folding, every step I do— requires the previous step to have succeeded. So, and folding itself also— demands a lot in terms of generalization. Because back then, if we were folding T-shirts, then we also— couldn't just fold one size or just one type of T-shirt. So actually, at the time we could simultaneously validate our— quality, our generalization, and our speed. Back then, it was from— from folding T-shirts that we derived some generalization recipes in terms of garment diversity and environment diversity, we actually arrived at some better conclusions about recipes. How did you prepare the data for the clothes-folding scenario to help it generalize better? Do you have know-how in this area? Actually, I think at the end of the day when it comes to post-training, it's really all a question of recipe. So actually, back then we roughly knew— for example, from what we saw at CoRL— this zero-shot T-shirt thing— back then we would actually test out in how many environments we needed to collect T-shirt folding data before we could zero-shot generalize in a new environment, that thing. Actually, we thought it would take a lot, but later we found that maybe just ten-plus environments were actually enough. Right, so T-shirts were the same— the T-shirts themselves, different sizes, different styles, different fabrics, different materials and so on, actually also have a certain— they also generalize to a certain extent. So at the time, in this direction, given the state of pre-training back then, we roughly probed out a ceiling for post-training, I guess. So can I roughly understand it this way— that based on the— pre-training capabilities at the time, you found a direction that could maximize— a direction where robots could get the most work done? Yes, exactly. And over the past year, this direction— with the breakthroughs in technology— has it changed? Has it changed at all? Actually, I think the most fundamental change is that the amount of data needed for post-training keeps getting smaller. And this actually, for us— it wasn't surprising at all. Actually, once we hit the post-training ceiling back then, after that we roughly knew, oh— the most important thing now should be pre-training. Did you later spend a few more months on deployment? Really going into the actual scenarios— after you had the demo? Yes. We did spend a few months on deployment. Which months were you doing deployment? Roughly June to September. Was deployment hard? Hard, very hard. Let me just take napkin folding as an example. Actually, with napkin folding itself, what everyone saw DYNA-1 do— the napkin folding wasn't a complete fold because it was something we, after discussing— after discussing with the client, an— intermediate form. Actually, at the time, folding the napkin required five folds and we only did four folds, and the last fold was left to the waiters because that last fold was very hard. Because after folding it over— because by then the napkin was already very thick and very small. So after the final fold it needed to be flattened out. If it wasn't flattened, it would— it would spread open. So between those two layers— there was this issue. At the time we only— when we only did four folds, when we went to deploy, the client started to complain. They had accepted it at the time, but in actual operation they were very unhappy because it wasted a lot of time. That's the first thing. Second, we actually found at the time that just folding the napkins well wasn't enough. Because in a day we needed to produce 1,300 napkins. This client of ours is very big— a very, very large client in the restaurant industry. They have a huge volume of customers every day. So they needed 1,300 napkins folded per day. And we found that DYNA-1 at that time could only fold 850 in 24 hours. Actually, at the time we— our speed at the time wasn't up to standard. So on the speed front we did a lot of research and finally met the standard. But after meeting the standard, we later discovered a very big problem, which was that every time we folded a napkin, although our model was very robust, there were some corner cases that we could resolve, but resolving them took a really long time. So actually, the most common problem back then was that we— sometimes our robot would grab— accidentally grab two napkins. Actually, even human hands sometimes accidentally grab two napkins. But after grabbing two napkins, it would take us about two minutes to resolve it. Normally, folding one napkin only takes 20 seconds. So every time it grabbed two napkins, our output for that day— would drop significantly. So if— if 10% of them were double folds back then, we definitely wouldn't hit the target that day. So at the time we were thinking how to use reinforcement learning and some other approaches to let the model solve this problem on its own. So at the time we were very firm that everything needed— everything had to be solved by the model. Actually, this thing itself is something pre-training can't help you solve. Because accidentally grabbing two napkins— because accidentally grabbing two napkins leading to decreasing output— this is very hard for pre-training to solve. So at the time we also discovered this. And then later we actually found that after folding at the restaurant, we had to put them in a bin. And when putting them into the bin, the pattern was very random, very inconsistent. So after you put them in, you'd— people who didn't know would come over and look and say these aren't folded properly, just a messy pile of napkins. And so at that point we had to start conditioning the model so that— first put it in the first position, the second position, the third position, placing them neatly in order. Have you adjusted the ratio of pre-training and post-training for this? Actually, this really comes down to — it's a pre-training problem. Because here, if you want to complete an entire workflow, then at every step of the workflow — that is, in every single task — there's a certain success rate and failure rate. So to complete the whole workflow, that's really like 99 times 99 times — it's a compound effect, it's an — it's an exponential process, where you — you — we need to do every task well, and we need to know how to solve every corner case of each task. And then when you chain them together, it can keep running continuously. The problem itself is very hard. It's really a pre-training problem at its core. The robot and model about to be released (not yet released at the time of recording; since released). Right, yes. From DYNA-1 to DYNA-2, what would you say the company's technical accumulation is? A whole lot. It's really hard to sum up in just a few sentences. But I think the core of it can be grouped into three systems: system two, system one, and system zero. Across all three systems, we've had really, really big improvements. First, system two is the embodied reasoning layer. It's basically the model's understanding of the physical world — whether it's the physical properties, or the state of what's happening right now, or even the understanding of semantics — it has a very strong understanding, so it can know what we're doing right now, which stage of the task we're currently at, and what our next step should be. And based on this understanding, it issues language commands to our system one. And system one, at its core, is really a world action model. After the world action model receives the language, how do we convert language into actions? And then at system zero, it's really about how we take the motion — the trajectory we want to output — and from the motion-control side, how we use better reinforcement learning methods to achieve fast, smooth, and accurate motions, rendered on the hardware. In all of this, have you observed signs of generalizability? On system one and system two, there are really, really strong signs of generalization. How far can it generalize? Actually, the dimensions of generalization are a very hard topic to explain in itself. Because, actually, the two most intuitive dimensions are very similar to humans. Say I teach you how to do something — I actually only have two ways to teach you. First, I demonstrate how to do the thing. Second, I use language to describe how to do the thing. So in the process of generalization, when you measure the dimensions of generalization, you can really just expand along these two dimensions. So on the demonstration dimension, it's how many demonstrations before you learn it. And at this level, what we've found is that for some very new tasks, with our current pre-training capabilities, it can already learn from just a few demonstrations. And the second is language steering capability, meaning I tell you — I use language to tell you to do something. Can the model do it? In this direction, we've actually also found a very strong language sensitivity increase. As for these details, we'll go into them in depth when we do the release. What signs of scaling law did you find in pre-training? We found that, when it comes to using human egocentric data, on a pre-training set of one million hours, we found a very strong scaling law itself. I think for the industry this should be a very important milestone, because over the past one to two years, for the vast majority of cases, the intelligence ceiling — this intelligence ceiling has been really hard to break through, because everyone says data is the biggest bottleneck. So back then, the first-principles thinking we did was: if we want to reach a GPT-3, GPT-4 robot foundation model moment, we need to take the lowest-quality data and extract from it what can scale, rather than scaling a high-quality dataset. This debate — actually, last year I had a very different conclusion, because when DYNA-1 was built, fundamentally it used the highest-quality data. And back then we found that we ran tons and tons of AB tests, and we found that the highest-quality data itself could train the best model. So at the time I told the team: you have to go all in on high quality data. And then we just kept grinding at it. But then I talked with a lot of people in the industry working on LLMs — Video Generation models as well. Honestly, at the time I was selectively hearing what I wanted to hear. They would say that when they were doing pre-training, they ran some ablation studies, and after taking some data out, they found their pre-training got better. But this — so at the time, using these points, I would tell the team: this is their observation, so high quality data is what matters most. But later on, when we actually started doing workflows, we found that what matters most is whether the model itself — whether we can give it the most diverse, broadest human experience. And then, once we reach internet scale data, doing ablation studies and distilling the data only becomes meaningful then. Before that premise is even met, doing too much quality optimization — I don't think that's necessarily first-principles thinking, at least when you look at this from the language model perspective. So after we had this shift in thinking on this front, we started to think about: what will the form of data look like three, five, ten years from now? What will the ultimate form of robot data look like? We thought about it over and over, and we couldn't think of anything better than egocentric or exocentric — that is, egocentric (first-person view data) and exocentric (third-person view data). We believe these will become the ultimate form of data, because, for example, if we were to bet on teleop, or bet on UMI, or bet on some form of data that isn't egocentric, I feel like we'd be, to some extent, betting that robot hands won't work. But we think the industry consensus is that robot hands themselves are an engineering problem, not a research problem. So, when we bet on egocentric, there was a certain — we would think about it on a 5-10 year horizon, and in the end we decided this is the largest dataset, and also the dataset of the ultimate form. So at that point we went all in on this model — on egocentric. So now you think more data matters more than higher-quality data? Is all of your data real-robot data? Right now we also have some simulation data. I think the data mixture — I mean, I don't think there's any need to declare whether I'm in the simulation camp or the real-robot camp. I think it all comes down to the training objective — what your goal is, what kind of model you want to train. Every model actually reflects its founder's and its team's different thinking embedded in it. For example, OpenAI back then was consumer application all the way, so their models back then, their early models, were more consumer friendly. How do you see the relationship between the robot body and the brain? I think both are very important. The brain is the robot's floor, and I think the body is the robot's ceiling. That's — that's actually the opposite of what many people think. Others might think the brain is the ceiling and the body is the floor. The brain determines whether something can be done at all. The hardware determines how well it can actually be done. That's actually my view. Because whether something can or can't be done is determined by the brain. And how well it can be done — that's actually, to some extent, determined by the hardware and motion control. Like, say I fold 1,000 — I can fold 1,000 napkins a day. Actually, if within the capabilities of data, motion control, and hardware, if it's possible, if it allows it, my movements can go faster, my movements can be more precise. So I think both are extremely important. And I think the relationship between brain and body is really, really tight — very hard to pull apart and analyze, because sometimes, because the body itself has video — meaning your camera, your perception, and your motion control. And the data you collect — if you look at it from the teleop data side, it's like — take motion control itself: data we collect from motion control — with two different motion control algorithms, you can train two very different models. One might be super smooth, really elegant, and the other might be a really terrible robot. So I find it hard to imagine truly doing B2B deployment scenarios if you don't own the robot body itself. It's very hard to hit their quality, speed, and efficiency. So I think both are — So do you think the brain should drive the body, or the body should drive the brain? I think the brain drives the body. The brain — this is actually a common dynamic in our team: the hardware spec has always been defined by the research team. For example, how many FPS does our camera need? Do I need global shutter or rolling shutter? And the camera angle — how wide does it need to be? All of this is defined by the research team. So I think the body needs to be defined from a research perspective. So if the brain makes a leap forward, would this generation of hardware stop working? Would it have to entirely replace the existing hardware setup? Would that kind of situation happen? I don't think it's that the brain's improvement makes the hardware stop working — it's that after the brain improves, the hardware may hit its ceiling. Like, your hardware can no longer do what my brain wants to do. Like my brain — to use the simplest example, this is the simplest one: say originally, my brain can play against a world-class table tennis player, but my body can't keep up. Then I'm limited by the hardware. So I'm wondering — is going full-stack now too early, since the brain problem isn't solved yet? I don't think it's too early, because at its core, it's really a co-develop process. It's about what kind of hardware my embodiment itself needs to pair with. And as the brain evolves — after it evolves, my hardware will definitely keep iterating continuously. The hardware body needs at least three revisions before you can truly enter mass production, before you can truly enter real scenarios. And I think right now, the supply chain hasn't fully converged yet. So hardware itself still has a huge number of problems. If you wait three years to start doing the hardware, then you've lost three years of iteration. So by then, sure, the brain problem is solved, and then you go define the hardware — yes, at that point you might define better hardware, but that doesn't mean your hardware can be deployed at that point, can be mass-produced. Right. Will you switch the gripper for a dexterous hand? Yes. In which generation? Does it matter for your scenarios? For now, it doesn't matter. Microsoft CEO on its NEW Nvidia-Powered AI Super Laptop

Joanna Stern(224.0K)·24:07·2026-10-15
Satya NadellaCEO, Microsoft Joanna SternHost, Joanna Stern
TL;DRMicrosoft CEO Satya Nadella introduced the Nvidia-powered Surface Laptop Ultra and a newly redesigned Windows operating system built around AI agents, emphasizing hybrid local-cloud computing, strict agent sandboxing, and enterprise-grade security.
Scientific and Engineering Challenges: Satya Nadella acknowledges that complex runtime systems like AI agents present real scientific and engineering hurdles that the industry does not yet fully understand. - Pacing and Social Permission: The industry must take its time...

TL;DR

Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella introduced the Nvidia-powered Surface Laptop Ultra and a newly redesigned Windows operating system built around AI agents, emphasizing hybrid local-cloud computing, strict agent sandboxing, and enterprise-grade security.

AI Safety and Industry Pacing

  • Scientific and Engineering Challenges: Satya Nadella acknowledges that complex runtime systems like AI agents present real scientific and engineering hurdles that the industry does not yet fully understand.
  • Pacing and Social Permission: The industry must take its time ("pacing") to engineer safety controls, as the social permission to operate depends on building technology that serves and remains under the control of humanity.

Windows Re-engineered for AI Agents

  • Dual-Identity Architecture: Windows is redesigned to give both the human user and the AI agent separate identities, ensuring they are contained, governed, and permissioned independently.
  • Security Primitives (MXC Containers): Features the MXC container as a technical primitive to keep agent activity secure and isolated within its designated boundary.
  • Behavioral Monitoring via Defender: Nadella treats all AI models and agents as an "insider risk," utilizing Microsoft Defender for ongoing, real-time behavioral monitoring to flag and shut down unauthorized actions.
  • Least Privilege Access: Agents start with minimal permissions, allowing IT admins or users to gradually scale up autonomous privileges as trust is earned.

Local AI and Hardware Partnership

  • Distributed Intelligence: Microsoft’s Surface Laptop Ultra leverages a partnership with Nvidia (featuring the RTX Spark chip and up to 128GB of memory, starting at $2,600) to run powerful models locally.
  • Ubiquity via Hybrid Compute: Nadella states that the industry is entering a hybrid moment where users will not need to distinguish between local and cloud models—similar to how OneDrive syncs local and cloud file systems.
  • Energy and Offline Benefits: Running models locally via the laptop's power reduces reliance on massive data center energy and water consumption, while enabling agents to function without internet access.

Consumer AI and Form Factors

  • Unified Copilot Ecosystem: Microsoft offers a single Copilot framework spanning consumer and commercial franchises, including chat, co-work, code, and "autopilot" (which users can rename, such as Nadella's personal "SN Mo").
  • Everyday Productivity Sweet Spot: Microsoft focuses its consumer AI on practical tasks like organizing files, managing Outlook/CRM integrations, and streamlining complex processes like filing taxes.
  • Beyond the Laptop (Project Solara): Microsoft is prototyping agent-first form factors, such as "Project Solara," which includes an early prototype of a fingerprint-secured wearable computing badge designed for mobile workflows like nursing.

Reading the Youth Pulse

World Economic Forum(1.1M)·55:18·2.5K views·2026-10-15
AbdelQader AlSaqqafSpeaker, World Economic Forum Tiffany LeeSpeaker, World Economic Forum Kacha MahadumrongkulSpeaker, World Economic Forum
TL;DRGlobal youth are experiencing a high-tech, highly interconnected reality that established leaders consistently underestimate, leading to severe entry-level job shortages, systemic education-to-work mismatches, and a growing demand for distributed, partnership-driven decision-making over tokenistic consultation.
AI and Entry-Level Job Displacement: Established leaders understand AI disruption conceptually, but recent graduates experience devastating entry-level job shortages on the ground. Global Youth Employment Statistics: According to preliminary Youth Pulse surveys, 35% of youth...

TL;DR

Global youth are experiencing a high-tech, highly interconnected reality that established leaders consistently underestimate, leading to severe entry-level job shortages, systemic education-to-work mismatches, and a growing demand for distributed, partnership-driven decision-making over tokenistic consultation.

The Global Youth Job Market Crisis & AI Disruption

  • AI and Entry-Level Job Displacement: Established leaders understand AI disruption conceptually, but recent graduates experience devastating entry-level job shortages on the ground.
    • Global Youth Employment Statistics: According to preliminary Youth Pulse surveys, 35% of youth cite employment as a national priority across all age ranges and 7 out of 8 geographic regions.
    • The Demographic and Job Shortage Gap: A demographic dividend over the next 10 years will see 1.2 billion youth enter employment against only 400 million new jobs created—leaving an 800 million job shortage (Shogun Mahadumrongkul).
    • Global Youth Unemployment: 20% of youth (257 million people) across developed and developing worlds are currently neither trained nor employed (Shogun Mahadumrongkul).
    • Regional Disparities: In India, 28% of college graduates are unemployed, compared to a 3.8% unemployment rate for those unable to read or write, illustrating a severe mismatch between traditional education and market needs (Shogun Mahadumrongkul).
  • The K-Shaped Economy: Technology adoption is driving economic divergence, where those leveraging AI and data accrue benefits while those left behind exacerbate existing inequalities.
  • Youth Tech Adoption: 91% of youth are already utilizing and embedding AI into their lives, despite regional variations where South Asian tech adoption sits lower (around 60%) but focuses heavily on problem-solving (Sakshi Sharma).
  • Alternative Pathways & Solopreneurship: Traditional education no longer responds to professional needs, giving rise to alternative paths (apprenticeships, Thiel Fellowship, Horry and Dreesen Academy) and the rise of one-person companies and solopreneurship (Shogun Mahadumrongkul).

Institutional Engagement vs. Tokenistic Consultation

  • The Demand for Distributed Decision-Making: Preliminary 2027 Youth Pulse results show 81% of youth favor distributed decision-making, while only 11% support traditional hierarchies (Tiffany Lee).
  • Flaws of Standalone Initiatives: Established leaders rely on Band-Aid solutions and standalone programs that lack enduring change and lose momentum once political will or funding dries up (Tiffany Lee).
  • Holistic Youth Priorities: When youth advocate for issues like housing or urban planning, they view problems holistically—tying affordable housing to the presence of community centers, schools, hospitals, walkability, and safety (Tiffany Lee).

Grassroots Action by Global Shapers Hubs

  • Jaipur Hub (India):
    • Led the Gram Innovation Network Project (an Innovation Prize winner in 2025), establishing tech adoption centers in rural communities (Sakshi Sharma).
    • 50% of the population impacted at these centers are young students adopting AI and hardware through vocational training (Sakshi Sharma).
  • Bangkok Hub / Avant Labs (Southeast Asia):
    • Founded Avant Labs to run AI literacy bootcamps for high-schoolers and SME owners, helping them implement AI solutions and scale them locally (Shogun Mahadumrongkul).
  • Vancouver Hub (Canada):
    • Mobilized local youth engagement for city elections through experiential learning, walking tours, and candidate debates focused on housing, nightlife policies, and public transit (Tiffany Lee).

Institutionalizing Youth Empowerment: The UAE Model

  • A Young Nation's Foundation: The UAE is a 55-year-old nation that from its inception gave youth responsibility because it had no other choice, partnering with youth across their entire lifespan (Khalid Mohammed Al Nuaimi).
  • The Federal Youth Authority (FYP):
    • Manages youth across the 15 to 35 age range without silos, connecting government and private entities to ensure a seamless life experience (Khalid Mohammed Al Nuaimi).
    • Established over 10 years, featuring more than 400 youth councils and over 4,000 participating members (Khalid Mohammed Al Nuaimi).
  • National Youth Agenda 2031: Created by sitting 10 relevant government ministers down with youth from government and private sectors to co-create the strategy from day one (Khalid Mohammed Al Nuaimi).
  • Accountability Metrics: Government, local, and private entities are assessed and held accountable for youth empowerment via strict metrics, including the presence of youth councils, percentage of youth in leadership positions, and direct integration into decision-making rooms (Khalid Mohammed Al Nuaimi).
  • High-Level Political Inclusion: 33% of UAE cabinet members were appointed under the age of 35, proving proximity to decision-making and co-creation with national leadership (AbdelQader AlSaqqaf, Khalid Mohammed Al Nuaimi).

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Global Workforce Demographics: In the next 10 years, 1.2 billion youth will enter employment against only 400 million new jobs created, generating an 800 million job shortage (Shogun Mahadumrongkul).
  • National Workforce Demographics (South Asia): Over 300 million young people under the age of 15 will enter the workforce in South Asia in the next 5 years (Sakshi Sharma).
  • Economic Uncertainty: No government, corporation, or individual knows the direction the job market is heading over the next 5 years due to AI's capacity to automate research, mathematical invention, and job functions (Shogun Mahadumrongkul).

Notable Quotes

"Established leaders are understanding this from a conceptual perspective and a high-level perspective. Whereas young leaders, especially recent graduates, are applying to jobs every day and experiencing the devastating effects of lack of entry-level jobs caused by AI disruption." — Shogun Mahadumrongkul
"Meaningful inclusion needs to include established leaders learning to champion young people’s ideas and going beyond just representation." — Tiffany Lee
"We’re moving into a world where for the first time and more than ever that young people coming into the workforce are sometimes more equipped than well-established people who are already within those institutions." — Khalid Mohammed Al Nuaimi

We're talking about a nation that started by giving youth the responsibility to lead. Not because we wanted to, but because we didn't have a choice at the time. And I think many of the lessons that we learn here in the UAE specifically about young people, is to make sure that not only do we work on giving them incentives, not only do we work on seeing them as people we have to empower, but also finding an avenue to partner with youth throughout the entire journey. And I think that's exactly what we do in the Federal Youth Authority. So, when we talk about youth, especially nowadays, we need to look across their entire lifespan. 20 years ago when we used to look at their lifespan, we used to look at a life that was from home to school to university to work with a small group of friends, with a with a small family, with a a close-knit community that they would talk to. Even parents would say, "I know exactly who my children's friends are and I know who I have to look out for." But now, when we're talking about the youth journey, we're talking about everyone, whether it is in your local community or in the Far East or Far West that's trying to get a piece of your attention as a young person. That's trying to, in one way, influence you towards a certain behavior, to showcase their culture to you, to speak to you throughout not only your physical location, but your virtual location. So, the the world that is moving very fast, the world that is influencing youth at a much larger pace. And that is why what we do in the Federal Youth Authority is we ensure that the way the government communicates with youth is not through silos. We don't say from high school, "Here you go university, I'm done." We don't say from universities to work, we hand over and we say our job is done. We need to make sure that if youth have a journey and we count youth from the age of 15 to 35 in the UAE, then we need to make sure that we stick together with them from the beginning of their journey to the end of their journey and make sure they have a seamless life experience by connecting all of the relevant government entities, private entities, and institutionalizing youth empowerment. I think first of all you mentioned you asked the question to Tiffany which is how long does it take, and the journey we've been on in the UAE to institutionalize youth participation has taken about 10 years. And in these past 10 years, since about 2016, we've launched many different initiatives, and I think when we talk about where we are today in the UAE, when we talk about the strategy of youth, where youth are going, we have what we call the National Youth Agenda. And in the National Youth Agenda 2031, the first thing we need to do is we need to talk about how this agenda was established, how it was created. And when we talk about co-creating for our strategies, co-creating for our youth, and a lot of cases as you mentioned, there's a checkbox that's there. There's a consultation that youth come in to the come in to the table after the decision has been made. So the way the UAE does it is quite different. So when we wanted to create the National Youth Agenda, we created all of the different pillars that we thought were relevant. Maybe 8 to 10. We brought all 10 relevant ministers and we sat those ministers down with youth from different government and private sector organizations to define what the next 7-year strategy is going to look like for youth. And that is where the national youth strategy came from. So in the UAE, we consider youth participation or integration as integration by design from the beginning of when we create our strategy. Second of all, when we talk about engagement, and this is something that you mentioned, and that reflects also to the Global Shapers program, which is our youth councils. So, we have 7 different types of youth councils in the UAE. We have youth councils which are on the city level, we have the youth councils which are on the government level entities. So every single ministry, federal agency, local agency will have its youth council in the UAE. And that is something that we've established over 10 years, it's taken a long time, but today we're talking about over 10 years, over 400 youth councils, over 4000 members who've been participating in these youth councils over the different cycles, and not only do we have those youth councils, but we assess our government entities, our local entities, our private sector entities, and we hold them accountable to how they've empowered youth throughout a set of very well-defined metrics. So, do you have a youth council? How many and what is the percentage of youth who are in leadership positions? How have they been integrated into the decision-making process? How do they join decision makers in the room when decisions are being made? So those are things that we don't only talk about but that we institutionalize. The Changing Physics of Cybersecurity and Drug Development with OpenAI President Greg Brockman

No Priors: AI, Machine Learning, Tech, & Startups(98.7K)·39:23·11.8K views·2026-10-15
Greg BrockmanPresident and Co-founder, OpenAI Sarah GuoHost, No Priors Elad GilHost, No Priors
TL;DROpenAI President and co-founder Greg Brockman discusses the rollout of GPT-6 to over a billion people, the resolution of nearly 400 century-old math problems by AI agents, and how proactive AI tools like "Dots" are transforming productivity and the future of work.
AI-Generated New Knowledge: Recent milestones in mathematics demonstrate that AI can definitively generate new knowledge rather than just retrieve it. - OpenAI recently published resolved results for nearly 400 significant, open mathematical problems. - Domain experts...

TL;DR

OpenAI President and co-founder Greg Brockman discusses the rollout of GPT-6 to over a billion people, the resolution of nearly 400 century-old math problems by AI agents, and how proactive AI tools like "Dots" are transforming productivity and the future of work.

Scientific Discovery & Mathematical Breakthroughs

  • AI-Generated New Knowledge: Recent milestones in mathematics demonstrate that AI can definitively generate new knowledge rather than just retrieve it.
    • OpenAI recently published resolved results for nearly 400 significant, open mathematical problems.
    • Domain experts reviewing the proofs note surprising and sophisticated applications of ideas and inter-domain connections rather than brute-force mechanical case-checking (unlike historical proofs like the Four Color Theorem).
  • Application to Biology and Drug Discovery: Scientific breakthroughs in mathematics and theoretical physics serve as a leading indicator for what is possible in biology, where deep learning physics (compute + data + algorithms) can accelerate discovery.
    • Drug discovery is highlighted as the most critical and impactful application of AI to improve human quality of life.
    • Neural networks are exceptionally well-suited to crack the "language of biology," enabling massive parallelism and dramatic shortening of a traditionally 15-year, $1.5 billion pipeline.
    • Non-biological bottlenecks in the drug pipeline—such as writing regulatory filings and clinical trial documentation—can also be streamlined by AI.

AI Agents, Form Factors, and the "No UI" Future

  • Proactive Productivity with Dots: OpenAI's "Dots" is an always-on, always-working proactive agent designed to track uncompleted tasks, monitor Slack and inbox feeds, manage scheduling conflicts, and anticipate user needs.
    • The form factor changes how users work by running continuously in the background even when the user stops working.
  • Consumer vs. Enterprise Market Pressures: While consumer assistants focus on mass-market scale, free-tier availability (such as ChatGPT's memory features for over 1.2 billion consumers), and features like payments and travel, workplace AI prioritizes peak intelligence, admin controls, security toggles, and paying higher compute costs for higher productivity returns.
  • Dynamic and Invisible User Interfaces: The ultimate UI for AI is no UI at all.
    • GPT-6 introduces intelligent, generative UI where the AI crafts custom, bespoke visual interfaces on the fly (such as flight booking options or assembly guides) tailored precisely to the moment and user request.

Cybersecurity and the Defender's Window

  • The Physics of Cybersecurity: Cybersecurity dynamics are shifting into a critical "defender's window" where peak frontier AI capabilities allow defenders to proactively find and fix deep-seated historical flaws in human-written code.
  • OpenAI Internal Security Response: During model releases, OpenAI pauses approximately 25% of its production engineers from feature work, assigning them exclusively to use peak frontier models to red-team and secure infrastructure until vulnerabilities are saturated.
  • Tech Debt and Code Resilience: Decades of internet infrastructure built for resilience rather than security have left immense technical debt (highlighted by single-maintainer open source vulnerabilities). AI enables formal verification of software and the generation of secure-by-default code from inception.

Human Agency and the Future of AGI

  • Keeping Humans in the Driver's Seat: OpenAI's core mission remains ensuring AGI benefits all of humanity by keeping humans in control and amplifying human agency.
    • Models are jagged and surpass humans in specific domains, but human judgment, values, and oversight function similarly to a CEO or board member directing specialized teams of domain experts.
  • Alignment via Debate: Techniques like debate and iterative amplification (conceptually discussed since 2017) train AIs to provide verifiable arguments and counter-arguments, ensuring humans can easily oversee complex outputs without needing to manually verify every detail.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Cyber vs. Software Spend: Security spend should vastly outstrip software spend as software becomes cheap and commoditized, making security, reliability, and resilience the true drivers of value — Greg Brockman

Notable Quotes

"In AI, like I have this this belief about how OpenAI operates... it never gets easier, you just go faster... every time you think you're at kind of peak sort of, you know, impact and how things are happening, you realize that there's actually another level that's possible." — Greg Brockman
"When I look at the world we're going to be in, where software in some ways is becoming like the barrier to entry, it's becoming so cheap... cyber spend, security spend should actually vastly outstrip software spend." — Greg Brockman
"The best UI is no UI... We're now in a world where the AI is molding itself much more to you, right? That the user interface is becoming exactly the thing you need at the moment you need it." — Greg Brockman
To ensure AGI benefits all of humanity, we think that humans should be firmly in the driver seat, should have control over the future, and this technology should amplify human agency. When I look at the world we're going to be in, where software in some ways is becoming like the barrier to entry, it's becoming so cheap. You don't just want software, right? You want software that is reliable, that is secure, that you can believe in. And so, cyber spend, security spend should actually vastly outstrip software spend. I think that people have not yet really internalized how much the physics around cybersecurity are going to change. There's an opportunity to end up with a much more secure world. The company building underwater drones for $50,000

Cheeky Pint(12.0K)·49:55·9.2K views·2026-10-15

TL;DRWill O'Brien, co-founder and president of Ulysses, joins John and Chris Sacca to discuss how his company is scaling autonomous underwater drones to $50,000 price points to revolutionize ocean conservation, maritime defense, and offshore energy infrastructure.

TL;DR

Will O'Brien, co-founder and president of Ulysses, joins John and Chris Sacca to discuss how his company is scaling autonomous underwater drones to $50,000 price points to revolutionize ocean conservation, maritime defense, and offshore energy infrastructure.

Guinness History and Technical Innovation

  • R&D Culture: Guinness established an in-house R&D team as early as the 1890s, operating similarly to Xerox PARC by hiring brilliant minds for undirected problem-solving.
  • The Student's T-Test:
    • Developed by William Sealy Gosset in the early 1920s to calculate standard deviation and means from small sample sizes (under 30).
    • Created because Guinness needed to test hundreds of hops for lipid/fat content without losing a few percent per batch, which impacted margins in the low-margin food-and-beverage industry.
    • Published under the pseudonym "The Student" in the *Journal of Mathematica* to hide innovation and margin improvements from competitors like Anheuser-Busch and Heineken.
  • Project ACORN: Developed in the 1980s (initially called Project Dynamite) to solve at-home Guinness consumption as pint prices rose from £1 to £1.50, resulting in the iconic widget.
  • Global Marketing Domination: Guinness won more Cannes Lions advertising awards than nearly any other company, championed the "Guinness is Good for You" campaign, the toucan mascot, and made Nigeria its top-performing market by producing localized films.
  • The 7Cs Quality Standards: A rigorous system covering crafted presentation, consistent freshness, counter visibility, correct gas, correct temperature, clean lines, and clean glassware.

Ulysses' Ocean Robot Stack & Architecture

  • Company Genesis: Founded three years ago by Will O'Brien and co-founders (including Colm, who leads manufacturing), initially focusing on seagrass restoration before expanding into defense and energy infrastructure. Lower Carbon Capital was the pre-seed lead investor.
  • The Vehicle Architecture:
    • Leviathan: An autonomous surface craft with a 2,000-nautical-mile range, Starlink integration, and wireless underwater charging capabilities.
    • Mako: An underwater robotic vehicle built from modular "Lego building blocks" (allowing swappable battery packs, rear thrusters, stereo 3D cameras, and sonar front modules).
  • Core Engineering Specs:
    • Operating Depth: Typically operates in the 1,500-meter range (where most economic activity occurs), crush-tested to 2,200 meters (full ocean technical depth is 6,000 meters).
    • Onboard Compute: Runs a full NVIDIA 5090 onboard with an Intel CPU, providing roughly 100 times more onboard compute than any other AUV.
    • Communications: Underwater comms are acoustical and low-bandwidth (~10 kilobytes per second over five kilometers), functioning like a dial-up modem ping.
  • Cost Disruption:
    • Base underwater vehicles (Mako) can be sold to navies for as cheap as $50,000, compared to standard US Navy AUVs costing roughly $5 million.
    • Surface vessels cost between $500,000 and $1 million.

Seagrass & Ecological Pragmatism

  • Seagrass Value Proposition:
    • Covers 2% of the seafloor but holds 20% of the ocean's carbon sink.
    • Approximately 20% of commercially important fish stocks rely on seagrass habitats.
    • Native species grow along Ireland, the UK, California, the East Coast, Australia, Virginia, Southern Florida, Sausalito, and the Great Barrier Reef.
  • Eco-Pragmatism: O'Brien describes himself as a pro-growth environmentalist who believes a flourishing future requires a consistently growing economy supported by a healthy biosphere.
  • Green Tape Friction: Regulatory "green tape" is cited as the single biggest barrier to environmental restoration, delaying massive projects like a planned seagrass initiative in Australia.

Defense, Offshore Energy, and Vertical Integration

  • Target Markets & Customers:
    • US Navy: Contracted for mine countermeasures (mine hunting) in regions like the Strait of Hormuz.
    • Offshore Wind: Mapping floating wind turbine power cables daily to prevent abandoned fishing lines from snagging on cables and trapping dolphins and whales; building robotic implements to cut away marine debris.
  • Vertical Integration Strategy:
    • Moving aggressively from off-the-shelf components (which comprised ~80% of generation-one vehicles) toward in-house manufacturing for scale.
    • Building proprietary software, sensor suites (acoustics teams building sonars in-house), and inertial navigation systems to bypass suppliers capped at 100–200 units per year.
  • The Economics of Autonomy: Traditional ocean operations require expensive surface ships ($100k+/day) and massive human crews constrained by union laws and seasickness; autonomous swarms eliminate human labor costs and operate 24/7 in harsh sea conditions.

Notable Quotes

*"Saltwater and the ocean is a very hostile environment. That’s harder than people realize when you're building a robot that lives in the ocean."* — Will O'Brien
*"We cover 2% of the seafloor, but seagrass holds 20% of the ocean's carbon... It's like your rapper's favorite rapper. It's the most slept-on plant, I think, out there. Coral is the very good-looking friend or something like that that gets all the attention, but seagrass is really who you should be talking to."* — Will O'Brien
*"Any numbnuts can put C4 on a vehicle and make it go boom. The very difficult, interesting technical question is, how do you build a general-purpose underwater robotic platform? And that's the thing that we think unlocks the ocean at scale."* — Will O'Brien

Also, our underwater vehicle, we wanted to build one kind of general-purpose form factor that could solve a lot of different problems. So there was some like pretty clear design decisions that we made at the start where our underwater vehicle Mako is made of these kind of Lego building blocks that make it up. So if you want to add, let's say, an extra 150 nautical miles of endurance and range, you just add extra battery packs because we knew some people would want that. Other people would want the vehicle to go faster. So you have a different rear thrust module. Some people want to take images underwater, so we have stereo cameras that can give you 3D imaging. Some people want sonar, you know, bathymetric maps, so we have a sonar front module. So... Yeah, there was a few very deliberate early design decisions which enabled us then when the US Navy comes knocking and says, hey, we've got some problems you'd like to work on, or offshore energy companies that... What Happens When AI Agents Can Spend Your Money?

a16z(301.0K)·50:31·7.3K views·2026-10-15
Terry AngelosExecutive, VGS Alex RampellGeneral Partner, a16z
TL;DRAI agents are transitioning from spectators to active market participants by securely accessing payment credentials, sparking a "wallet wars" race to connect agents with existing payment methods.
Infrastructure providers like VGS are facilitating this shift by issuing secure agentic tokens that enable AI transactions while preserving consumer protections, fraud checks, and merchant relationships. - The Core Role of VGS: Explained by Terry Angelos as securing credentials...

TL;DR

AI agents are transitioning from spectators to active market participants by securely accessing payment credentials, sparking a "wallet wars" race to connect agents with existing payment methods. Infrastructure providers like VGS are facilitating this shift by issuing secure agentic tokens that enable AI transactions while preserving consumer protections, fraud checks, and merchant relationships.

The Evolution of Payment Infrastructure & Vaulting

  • The Core Role of VGS: Explained by Terry Angelos as securing credentials for roughly one-third of all e-commerce by Gross Merchandise Value (GMV), serving major online merchants, neo-banks, and frontier AI labs by taking PCI DSS compliance completely out of scope.
  • The Shift from PANs to Network Tokens: Explained by Terry Angelos as moving away from vaulting raw 16-digit primary account numbers (PANs) toward domain-bound network tokens issued by Visa, Mastercard, AMEX, and Discover to enhance security on specific merchant domains.
  • Multi-Processor Sharding: Described by Alex Rampell and Terry Angelos as a requirement for merchants with over $1 billion in GMV, who typically use 3 to 5 payment processors to optimize costs, approval rates, and regional routing—multiplying the complexity of vaulting and card updates.
  • Card Turnover and Updates: Highlighted by Terry Angelos that about 25% of cards expire annually due to a standard four-year lifecycle, with an additional 5% turning over due to fraud, portfolio acquisitions (e.g., Capital One buying Discover), or network switches—complications that large, neutral vaults resolve automatically.

The Rise of Agentic Commerce & The Wallet Wars

  • Agents with Arms and Legs: Described by Terry Angelos as a major leap over the last 3 months where AI assistants (like Muse, Instinct, and Town) have evolved from passive recommendation tools to actors capable of completing end-to-end purchasing tasks (such as booking piano teachers or finding chandeliers) once granted safe payment access.
  • The Wallet Wars: Highlighted by Terry Angelos as consumer and merchant wallets rushing to integrate with AI agents based on consumer reach and merchant footprint:
    • Stripe Link: Reaches ~300 million consumers and ~1 million merchants.
    • Shopify Shop Pay: Connects with ~1.7 million merchants.
    • PayPal: Encompasses over 400 million accounts and ~30 million merchants.
  • Stripe as the "AOL" of Agentic Commerce: Compared by Terry Angelos to early internet walled gardens; Stripe handles transactions by issuing virtual one-time-use Visa cards when direct links to merchants (like Walmart) are absent, though this workaround creates friction with chargebacks and merchant re-engagement.

Open Standards, Agentic Tokens, and Merchant Friction

  • The New Agentic Token Standard: Detailed by Terry Angelos as a 16-digit tokenized credential issued via networks that allows wallets to hand safe parameters (such as spending caps and agent verification) to AI agents, preserving traditional chargeback mechanisms and post-purchase engagement.
  • VGS's Growth in Tokens: Noted by Terry Angelos that VGS went from zero agentic tokens 3 months ago to processing a few million tokens per week across agents, wallets, and merchants.
  • Merchant Pushback (Amazon vs. Walmart): Discussed by Terry Angelos and Alex Rampell as a strategic divide: Amazon blocks third-party agents like Muse to protect its high-margin advertising business and direct consumer eyeballs, whereas Walmart and SMBs under Shopify are more receptive or view agents as vital sales channels.

Transactional Security: The Three Checks of Commerce

  • Step 1 (Card Validity): Outlined by Alex Rampell as verifying whether a credit card number is structurally valid via checksums (mod 10 check) and checking with the issuing bank.
  • Step 2 (Identity & Ownership): Described by Alex Rampell as verifying the human or agent owner via cryptographic identity, similar to legacy practices like checking photo IDs and signatures for large purchases.
  • Step 3 (Sound Mind/Delegated Authority): Explored by Alex Rampell and Terry Angelos as verifying buyer intent and preventing errors or hallucinations (e.g., preventing an agent from buying the wrong hotel dates or unauthorized high-value items), with delegated authority and liability remaining largely unresolved.

Over-Hyped vs. Under-Hyped Trends

  • Over-Hyped (Financial Volatility): Dismissed by Terry Angelos as unlikely to cause bank runs or extreme volatility in savings deposits through automated yield-maximizing agents due to existing banking friction and bill-pay integrations.
  • Under-Hyped (Unmonetized Demand & Agent Savings): Claimed by Terry Angelos as the hidden expansion of GDP through AI agents continuously identifying consumer needs in group chats, alongside agents saving consumers money on forgotten subscriptions that can be redirected toward new, desired purchases.

Notable Quotes

"Stripe in many ways is like the AOL of Agentic Commerce... they've figured out how to make agentic commerce just work in a way that's a very kind of, you know, sort of, you know, walled garden approach." — Terry Angelos
"Vaults are going from single player to multiplayer, and that makes them a lot more valuable." — Terry Angelos

One is the digital version of your dead cow wallet, which is that it just stores the payment credential. And when you go to a merchant, you hand that credential to the merchant. All right, that's kind of what Apple Pay does, right? When I shop at Apple Pay, or so when I shop at a store with Apple Pay, Apple is effectively giving my Capital One Visa card effectively tokenized in the wallet to the merchant. The merchant receives this credential and it decides whether it processes it through Chase or Adyen or Stripe or Braintree. Then you have wallets that actually take care of the transaction for you. So think about Shop Pay or Link or PayPal where the wallet is in fact transacting with me, collecting the money from me, and remitting that back to the merchant. And what's happening with agentic commerce is we're sort of getting this sort of blend of the two, and it's such a new ecosystem that it actually has parallels for me back to the early days of the internet. And for those people who were around in the sort of AOL era, Stripe in many ways is like the AOL of agentic commerce. And I mean that as a compliment in the sense that they've figured out how to make agentic commerce just work in a way that's a very kind of walled garden approach. So, a few examples here would be if I use Stripe Link, Link's only available at a million merchants. So, it's great if in fact the item that I want is available at one of those million merchants, but very often it's available at some place where there isn't a Stripe Link connection. So, a good example was I think two weeks ago I had this event at my house. I wanted to buy some outdoor lights and I asked Instinct to go and help me find these lights, and I found the lights at Walmart, which makes a ton of sense. Walmart has lots of inventory. And the agent went, loaded the cart, and when it came time to pay, Link didn't have a connection to Walmart. It, Link has my card, but it can't just give my card over to the agent and over to Walmart. So, Stripe did something pretty interesting. They purchased the chandeliers on my behalf. They issued their own, actually this is like a Visa virtual card, one-time use card, made the purchase, and then they charged me, Terry, at a second transaction. This is a little bit how like DoorDash works today. You know, you spend 100 bucks at DoorDash and then the dasher goes and pays the restaurant. But in the case of this Walmart transaction, this is the point about it sort of being the AOL, it worked just great. Like I actually, the item showed up, the transaction went through, I was charged the right amount. But if there's a problem with the chandelier, typically I call up my bank, Capital One, and I say, hey, I had this transaction with Walmart, something went wrong, and they say, well, we don't see a Walmart transaction here. We see a Stripe. We just see a Stripe transaction. And in fact, you will find this pretty funny. So, they gave them a phone number to call whoever this Link merchant is. The person called them, it went straight to the cell phone of a Stripe engineer because Stripe's not set up to run a chargeback. Well, this is the main, one of the reasons why Visa and Mastercard have such impregnable positions is because they intermediate everything and they set the rules and chargebacks are very important. Yeah. Right? Like what if I paid for something? It's a trust mechanism. I pay you for something. I pay you for lights. You don't deliver the lights. Like, oh shoot, I don't have money anymore. What do I do? No, no, no. You actually have rules by which I can kind of lodge a complaint, and then there's a central intermediary that helps adjudicate those complaints in a way that is more or less fair that both parties agree on. I mean, it's part of the problem. Like this is why crypto's cool, but like it's a bearer instrument. I pay you with crypto. I'm never getting that back. Which again can be a bug or a feature, but for your lights example, like what if the lights don't work? You want to have Visa and Mastercard in the middle. That's high utility actually even for the merchant because now I am much more willing to buy with the merchant knowing that if they don't actually deliver a product that works, I can get my money back. Yeah. So, in fact, this is like I think the right sort of direction of travel from the AOL ecosystem to the open standards ecosystem. The card networks have actually come out with a new type of standard. They're called agentic tokens that will allow a wallet like a Stripe Link or a Shop Pay or a PayPal to hand over a credential to the agent in a safe way and then have the agent hand it to the merchant in such a way that when that Walmart transaction occurred, it's my card. In fact, like one sort of addendum to the Walmart transaction, after I bought the chandelier, everything was fine. I got an email from Walmart to say, hey Terry, you paid for delivery. It was like $6.99. Why don't you sign up to Walmart Plus and here's an offer. If you sign up today, we will credit the delivery toward your Walmart Plus subscription. So, I said, great. Part of the norms of e-commerce is that Walmart spends a lot of money to get me as a customer and then they have the right to try to have a relationship with me, upsell me, cross-sell me. And in this case, because we were using the sort of AOL version, they weren't able to easily allow me to subscribe to this new service. With the new agentic standards, what should happen is if any of the wallets don't have a relationship with the merchant, but they have my card on file, my Capital One Visa card, that should be tokenized with the agent. There's a whole process by which the agent says, I'm about to make a transaction, I'm a verified agent, I'm going to spend no more than $100. That the token is nothing more than a new 16-digit number. We give that safely to the agent. Agent gives it to Walmart, and then Walmart runs the transaction, but it's my card that is being charged, and that preserves this ability to have a follow-on transaction. It preserves all of the rules around chargebacks. And so, 3 months ago, this was at zero. It did not exist. VGS is by far the largest provisioner of these agentic tokens. We do it for the agents. We do it for the wallets. We do it for some of the merchants, and this was zero 3 months ago. Now we're doing a few million of these tokens per week. So, I expect that as we make this transition from the walled garden to the sort of open standards, this will be the way that agentic commerce evolves. Yeah, I mean it's one of the reasons why this kind of long, I mean this as a positive, but like VGS has a very boring business. Why is it boring? Because we have a vault. Zelenskyy on Trump's relationship with Putin | FT Interview

Financial Times(1.5M)·27:59·203.7K views·2026-10-15
Volodymyr ZelenskyyPresident, Ukraine
TL;DRUkrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy criticized the Trump administration's pressure to halt strikes on Russian energy infrastructure while U.S. intelligence sharing and Starlink speeds were restricted, warning that deals with Moscow only fund continued aggression.
He also asserted that Donald Trump harbors an instinctive affinity for autocrats and the super-wealthy. Intelligence and Communications Interference: Starlink Slowdowns: Volodymyr Zelenskyy stated that Starlink speeds were deliberately throttled by SpaceX/American will...

TL;DR

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy criticized the Trump administration's pressure to halt strikes on Russian energy infrastructure while U.S. intelligence sharing and Starlink speeds were restricted, warning that deals with Moscow only fund continued aggression. He also asserted that Donald Trump harbors an instinctive affinity for autocrats and the super-wealthy.

U.S. Pressure and Starlink Restrictions

  • Intelligence and Communications Interference:
    • Starlink Slowdowns: Volodymyr Zelenskyy stated that Starlink speeds were deliberately throttled by SpaceX/American will across Ukrainian territory and the capital during critical military moments.
    • Logistical Impact: Supply drops of food and water to soldiers in gray zones had to shift entirely to high-speed domestic drones because slowed Starlink-dependent operations risked destruction.
    • Military-Level Threats: Volodymyr Zelenskyy noted that senior U.S. military officials threatened to block intelligence sharing if Ukraine did not stop attacking Russian oil refineries.
  • The Energy Ceasefire Breakdown:
    • Unilateral Halt: Volodymyr Zelenskyy agreed to a 3-day pause on striking Russian energy and diesel systems to support President Trump's proposed energy ceasefire.
    • Russian Retaliation: Despite the Ukrainian pause, Russian forces launched massive ballistic and drone attacks hitting civilian and energy infrastructure across Kyiv, Kharkiv, and Lutsk.
    • Resumption of Strikes: Following civilian deaths and renewed Russian bombardment, Volodymyr Zelenskyy instructed the Ukrainian military to resume responses against Russian energy assets.

Geopolitical Strategy and the Trump-Putin Relationship

  • Autocratic Affinity: Volodymyr Zelenskyy asserted that Donald Trump has an instinctive affinity for autocrats and the super-wealthy, noting Trump's public respect for leaders who hold power for decades.
  • Economic Motivations: Volodymyr Zelenskyy warned that U.S. eagerness to strike business and energy deals with Moscow generates funding that Russia converts directly into military production capacity.
  • The "Diesel Deal" Risk: Volodymyr Zelenskyy rejected deals that silence Ukrainian battlefield responses, comparing them to slippery slopes that could next lead to gas deals and increased funding for Russian weapon manufacturing.

European Security and Technology Sharing

  • Military Tech Exchange: Volodymyr Zelenskyy argued that European nations—specifically France, the UK, and the US—benefit heavily from co-production and real-world Ukrainian combat testing of advanced systems like Sampi air defense, Scalps missiles, and anti-drone defenses.
  • Budget Savings for Europe: Volodymyr Zelenskyy noted that Ukraine's battlefield experience saved European budgets billions of dollars on conventional tanks and armaments ill-suited for modern technological warfare.
  • Skepticism Toward Far-Right Figures: Addressing Marine Le Pen's proposal to halt funding for Ukraine after six months, Volodymyr Zelenskyy countered that European security relies on Ukrainian technological cooperation, and claimed Le Pen is not deeply versed in the details of reciprocal security benefits.
